```xml
  <svta:Click xmlns:svta="urn:svta:dash:sgai:2026"
              clickThroughUrl="https://advertiser.example.com/901"/>
</Period>
```

| Attribute | Required | Type | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `@clickThroughUrl` | yes | `xs:anyURI` | — | The destination the Player opens, or hands to the platform, when the viewer activates the click. |

| Child | Required | Cardinality | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| `<svta:ClickTracking>` | no | 0..n | One click-tracking URL as the element's text content. Fired on activation, not at a presentation time. |

**The obligation, stated positively and scoped.** A Player conformant
to this specification reads `@clickThroughUrl` and fires every
`<svta:ClickTracking>` URL once when the viewer activates the click.

**Why that scoping is the only one available.** The base specification
specifies no DASH client behaviour normatively — *"as DASH Client
operation is not specified normatively in this document, it is also
unspecified how a DASH Client conforms to a particular profile. Hence,
profiles merely specify restrictions on MPD and Segments rather than
DASH Client behaviour"* (DASH §8.1, NOTE 1) — so no construct, in any
namespace and under any profile, compels a Player that has never heard
of this specification. The scoping is not a weakening; it is the whole
of what any extension of DASH can promise.

Whether a ClickThrough carries click-tracking is the advertiser's
decision. A `<svta:Click>` with no `<svta:ClickTracking>` child is a
complete, conformant declaration: the Player opens the destination and
fires nothing.

```xml
<svta:Click xmlns:svta="urn:svta:dash:sgai:2026"
            clickThroughUrl="https://advertiser.example.com/landing">
  <svta:ClickTracking>https://tracker.example.com/click?ad=1</svta:ClickTracking>
  <svta:ClickTracking>https://thirdparty.example.net/c?id=1</svta:ClickTracking>
</svta:Click>
```

### 5.7 Application-level metadata

Generic creative metadata with no native carrier in the base
specification rides on four extension elements, each an OPTIONAL child
of `<svta:Candidate>`, at most once, carrying its value as element
text.

| Element | Required | Cardinality | Description |
|---|---|---|---|
| `<svta:AdSystem>` | no | 0..1 | Identifier of the ad system that produced the decision. |
| `<svta:AdTitle>` | no | 0..1 | Human-readable title of the creative. |
| `<svta:Advertiser>` | no | 0..1 | Human-readable identifier of the advertiser. |
| `<svta:UniversalAdId>` | no | 0..1 | A registry-scoped universal identifier of the creative. |

**Emitting and reading are both optional.** Nothing obliges an APS to
emit these elements or a Player to read them, and a Player that ignores
every one of them is conformant. What this specification fixes is that
the place exists and is named; no ad presentation depends on anyone
using it.

That is the one contrast worth drawing with §5.6: this carrier is
optional on both ends, while the ClickThrough carrier is read by a
Player conformant to this specification.

The universal ad identifier is carried here **at most on a best-effort
basis, and no carrier is mandated for it.** Its reconciliation role
sits on the decisioning side, where the upstream ad standards already
carry it; mandating it here would add a field implementations populate
inconsistently and no Player behaviour depends on.

### 5.8 The resolution request

The Player issues the resolution request as an HTTP GET against the
slot's `@uri`. Two independent sources contribute query parameters to
that URL: the Publisher's author-declared template (§5.8.1) and the
Player's own capability declaration (§5.8.2).

#### 5.8.1 Publisher-declared parameters

The Publisher MAY declare a query template through the base
specification's extended HTTP GET parameterisation, under the scheme
URI `urn:mpeg:dash:urlparam:2025`. The signalling is two separate
elements: an **empty descriptor** enabling the scheme at MPD level, and
a **`<RequestParam>`** element of type `ExtendedUrlInfoType` carrying
the template (DASH §I.3.1) `[inferred]`. The element is
`<RequestParam>`; the base specification declares no element named
`UrlParamInfo`. It *"may be present in elements such as but not limited
to MPD, Period, AdaptationSet, Representation, Preselection, or
EventStream"* (DASH §I.3.1), so authoring it inside a slot's own
`<EventStream>` scopes the template to the windows that stream
carries.

| Attribute | Required | Type | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `@queryTemplate` | yes, for a template that contributes query parameters | `xs:string` | — | The template. It *"shall contain one or more $<ParamIdentifier>$ template identifiers"* (DASH §I.2.2.2), which the Player substitutes with live values at resolution time. |
| `@includeInRequests` | yes | whitespace-separated token list | `segment` in the base specification | Which request types the template applies to. |

| Enum value (of `@includeInRequests`, as used here) | Description |
|---|---|
| `altmpd` | Scopes the template to requests for MPDs representing the alternative Media Presentation — that is, to the **linear** family's resolution requests. |
| `urn:svta:dash:request:sgai-resolution:2026` | Scopes the template to the **non-linear** resolution request of this specification: the GET a Player issues against an overlay or pause window's `@uri`. |

**Why a request-type URN of our own, and why it is legacy-safe.**
`altmpd` names alternative-MPD requests, and a non-linear resolution
request is not one. The value space of `@includeInRequests` admits a
request type named by its owner: its last row is *"a URN or tag URI,
where the request type semantics is understood by the client and
specified by the URN / tag URI owner. The client shall drop unknown
URIs from the @includeInRequests and @includeInHeaders strings prior to
processing them"* (DASH Table I.4). Naming the non-linear request with
a URN is therefore the base specification's own extension hook, and the
drop rule is what makes it safe: a client that does not know the URN
removes the token and processes the rest.

<!-- refine: v9-dash-conformance-audit.md#M7 -->
**Against a profile that enumerates the values it admits.** DASH
§8.13.2.7 lists the values that *"may appear in the @includeInRequests
attribute"* under the Advanced Linear profile — `mpd` and `mpdpatch`,
`segment` and `init`, `steering`, `callback`, `altmpd` and `mpdlink` —
and the URN of this specification is not among them. That enumeration
does not narrow the value space: Table I.4 defines the space
generically and admits an owner-specified URN inside it, and a
profile's list of admitted values constrains what an MPD under that
profile declares rather than what the mechanism accepts. A Publisher
declaring the Advanced Linear profile therefore authors the URN
alongside `altmpd`, and the drop rule leaves a client that knows
neither the profile nor the URN unaffected.

A template declares both tokens only if it wants both kinds of request
parameterised; they are independent.

<!-- refine: v9-dash-conformance-audit.md#N2 -->
```xml
<MPD xmlns="urn:mpeg:dash:schema:mpd:2011"
     xmlns:svta="urn:svta:dash:sgai:2026"
     type="static" mediaPresentationDuration="PT42M" minBufferTime="PT2S"
     profiles="urn:mpeg:dash:profile:isoff-live:2011">
  <Period id="1" start="PT0S">
    <EventStream schemeIdUri="urn:svta:dash:event:sgai-overlay:2026"
                 timescale="1000">
      <Event id="201" presentationTime="600000" duration="30000">
        <svta:OverlayPresentation uri="https://aps.example.com/decision/overlay"
                                  maxDuration="30000"
                                  allowedLayouts="overlay-corner"/>
      </Event>
      <RequestParam includeInRequests="urn:svta:dash:request:sgai-resolution:2026"
                    queryTemplate="video_profile=$urn:mpeg:dash:state:video$&amp;session_id=$urn:mpeg:dash:state:cmcd#sid$"/>
    </EventStream>
    <AdaptationSet id="1" mimeType="video/mp4" codecs="avc1.4d401f">
      <Representation id="v1" bandwidth="2500000" width="1280" height="720"/>
    </AdaptationSet>
  </Period>
  <EssentialProperty schemeIdUri="urn:mpeg:dash:urlparam:2025"/>
</MPD>
```

This specification extends that mechanism in no way beyond naming one
additional request type.

#### 5.8.2 Player-declared capability parameters

This specification reserves a set of query-parameter names the Player
MAY attach to the resolution request, stating what its device can
render. Which of them travel is the Player's decision at runtime; no
declaration by any other actor is required first.

| Parameter | Required | Type | Default | Description |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `sgaiVideoDecoders` | no | unsigned integer, `1` or greater | — (absent means undetermined, §5.8.4) | How many video decoders the device runs concurrently, counting the one presenting the primary content. |
| `sgaiImageOverlay` | no | boolean (`true` \| `false`) | — (absent means undetermined) | Whether the device composites a still image on top of, or alongside, playing video. |
| `sgaiHtmlOverlay` | no | boolean (`true` \| `false`) | — (absent means undetermined) | Whether the device composites an HTML surface on top of, or alongside, playing video. |

**Why these are not carried by the author-declared template.** The
mechanism of §5.8.1 is the base specification's upstream channel for
this class of request, and it misses on two independent axes. Its
payload is session **state**: *"there is a need to express knowledge of
the current state of the player"* (DASH §I.4.1), and its vocabulary is
what is playing and what has run — codecs and bandwidth, selected
language, protection, delivery keys, per-event execution counters. No
parameter in it names a decoder count or a compositing surface. And the
template is authored by the **content author**: `@queryTemplate`
*"provides URL parameters template information. This string shall
contain one or more $<ParamIdentifier>$ template identifiers"* (DASH
§I.2.2.2), so a Player cannot add an axis to a template it did not
write. Either miss alone would be enough.

The parameters are **inputs about the device** — statements of what it
supports — and not conclusions about which ad experiences can be
served. Deriving the second from the first is the APS's work.

```
GET /decision/overlay?slot=mid1&sgaiVideoDecoders=1&sgaiImageOverlay=true&sgaiHtmlOverlay=false
```

The set is minimal against its acceptance test, which is that the three
axes separate the five device classes of §3.4:

| Declaration | Class |
|---|---|
| `sgaiVideoDecoders=2`, `sgaiImageOverlay=true`, `sgaiHtmlOverlay=true` | D1 |
| `sgaiVideoDecoders=2`, `sgaiImageOverlay=false`, `sgaiHtmlOverlay=false` | D2 |
| `sgaiVideoDecoders=1`, `sgaiImageOverlay=true`, `sgaiHtmlOverlay=true` | D3 |
| `sgaiVideoDecoders=1`, `sgaiImageOverlay=true`, `sgaiHtmlOverlay=false` | D4 |
| `sgaiVideoDecoders=1`, `sgaiImageOverlay=false`, `sgaiHtmlOverlay=false` | D5 |

**Why a boolean rather than a surface list.** A list has no way to say
"no surfaces at all" that is distinguishable from an empty value, and
§5.8.3 has a parameter the Player will not populate omitted rather than
emptied — so an empty list and an absent parameter would collapse into
one another, and D5 would be indistinguishable from a Player that
declared nothing. Two booleans keep "declares no image surface" and
"says nothing about image surfaces" apart.

**Why no fourth axis.** A fourth would have to separate two classes
these three already separate, and the table above shows none remains.

#### 5.8.3 Sending, omitting and extending

- Sending a reserved parameter is **OPTIONAL**. A Player conformant to
  this specification MAY send all of them, some of them, or none.
- Where the Player has no value for a reserved parameter, or does not
  disclose its value, it **omits the parameter entirely** rather than
  sending it with an empty or placeholder value.
- A parameter the Player attaches that is not one of the reserved names
  carries a vendor prefix of the form `x-<vendor>-`, so that reserved
  names added in a later edition stay free.
- The reserved names are reserved on the resolution request as a whole.
  A Publisher authoring a `<RequestParam>` query template (§5.8.1)
  draws its parameter names from outside the reserved set, so the two
  sources compose on one URL without collision: the Player appends its
  own parameters to the query string the template produced.

#### 5.8.4 What an absent parameter means

A reserved parameter absent from the resolution request means its value
is **undetermined**: the Player did not determine it, or did not
disclose it. Absence does not assert that the device lacks the
capability.

**The APS answers without any of them.** A request carrying none of the
reserved parameters is answered with the candidates' options
unnarrowed — the same document an APS emits when it holds no device
view at all — so a Player that declares nothing receives the full
ordered list and resolves the choice itself.

**How an APS resolves an undetermined value is its own decision.** An
APS that resolves conservatively, emitting no option that depends on
the undetermined axis, and an APS that assumes the most capable case
are **both conformant**, and they emit different documents to the same
Player. What does not vary is that the Player checks whatever arrives
before rendering it (§4.5.3).

### 5.9 Declaring the pause-delivery metric

Pause-ad delivery is measured with the base specification's play-list
metric, and this specification defines no metric of its own. It is *"A
list of playback periods"*, where a playback period is *"the time
interval between a user action and whichever occurs soonest of the next
user action, the end of playback or a failure that stops playback"*
(DASH Annex D.4.6). Two of its fields carry what a pause slot needs: an
entry's `starttype`, whose value space includes `Resume`, and a
rendered stretch's `stopreason`, which includes `UserRequest` and
`Rebuffering`.

The paused interval is therefore **derived**: the interval between the
end of a playback period whose last rendered stretch stopped on
`UserRequest` and the `start` of the next entry whose `starttype` is
`Resume`. Against it, the duration the pause ad occupied is the filled
fraction. A period that stopped on `Rebuffering` is a stall, not a
pause opportunity.

The Publisher requests the metric with the `<Metrics>` element, on the
**main MPD**:

```xml
<Metrics metrics="PlayList" reportingInterval="PT60S">
  <Range starttime="PT0S"/>
</Metrics>
```

<!-- refine: v9-spec-validation.md#F9 -->
A Single-Period Static MPD admits no MPD-level `Metrics` element (DASH
§8.15.2), so the main MPD is the only place this
declaration lives.

**How a measurement reaches anyone is out of scope**, and the base
specification takes the same position about its own metrics: *"This
document does not define mechanisms for reporting metrics; however, it
does define a set of metrics and a mechanism that may be used by the
service provider to trigger metric collection and reporting at the
clients, if a reporting mechanism is available"* (DASH §5.9.1). This
specification defines the quantity and inherits that silence about
transport.

## 6. Interfaces

This chapter describes the exchanges a Player takes part in: the messages, their
order, and what each participant is answerable for on each hop. The syntax of
the constructs named here is in §5; this chapter walks the flows they carry.

Three flows exist, one per opportunity family: the linear flow, the non-linear
overlay flow, and the pause flow. All three share one shape — the Publisher
declares a window in the main MPD, the Player resolves that window against the
APS, and the APS answers with a **resolution document** — and differ in what
triggers the resolution, what the document contains, and what happens to the
primary content while the ad is on screen.

### 6.1 The linear flow

A linear slot takes over the primary content surface for a bounded stretch of
the timeline. The base specification defines the construct this flow reuses:
*"An alternative Media Presentation is a presentation that replaces the main
Media Presentation at a certain point on the media timeline for a duration of
time. After the playback of the alternative Media Presentation is complete, the
playback is continued with the main Media Presentation. A key use case for this
is advertisement, where alternative Media Presentation represents the inserted
advertisement"* (DASH §5.16.1).

```
                         primary content (Publisher CDN)
                                      ^
                                      | (3) GET segments
   +--------------+  (1) GET main MPD |
   |  Publisher   |<------------------+----------+       (8) GET ad segments
   |  (encoder +  |                   |          |------------------------> ad CDN
   |   packager   |--(2) MPD + event--|  Player  |
   |   + CDN)     |     stream        |          |--(10) GET beacon --> tracking
   +--------------+                   +----+-----+                        endpoint
                                        |     ^
                    (5) GET <@uri>?<q>  |     |  (7) 200 + resolution document
                                        v     |
                                     +-----------+   (6) ad decisioning
                                     |    APS    |<----------------------> ADS
                                     | (adapter) |    (decision document)
                                     +-----------+
```

1. The Player fetches the main MPD and begins playing the primary content.
2. The MPD carries the opportunity as an `<Event>` inside an `<EventStream>` of
   the insertion or the replacement scheme. The `<Event>` holds an
   `<InsertPresentation>` or a `<ReplacePresentation>` child that declares the
   APS endpoint in `@uri`, the slot cap in `@maxDuration`, and the
   earliest-resolution offset in `@earliestResolutionTimeOffset`.
3. The Player fetches primary segments and plays the main timeline.
4. The Player computes the **Earliest Resolution Time** as the event's
   `@presentationTime` minus `@earliestResolutionTimeOffset`, and picks a
   randomised instant between that time and the event's `@presentationTime`.
   Randomising the instant spreads the resolution load of a viewer population
   that would otherwise arrive at the APS together.
5. At that instant the Player issues an HTTP `GET` against the event `@uri`,
   extended with the query the Publisher declared and with the reserved
   capability parameters the Player chooses to populate (§6.4).
6. The APS obtains a decision from the ADS and converts it. That exchange is
   bilateral between those two actors and outside this specification; §6.6
   describes one such conversion illustratively.
7. The APS answers `200` with the linear resolution document: a `ListMPD`
   carrying one `<Period>` per ad, each holding an `<ImportedMPD>`, or a
   single-period alternative MPD where the slot carries one ad.
8. The Player validates each candidate against the declarations of the window
   that served it, plays the surviving candidates in the order the document
   declares, and fetches the ad media through each sub-MPD from the ad CDN.
9. The Player enforces the cap, trimming the presentation at the boundary the
   cap sets for the family (§7.2).
10. The Player fires the beacons carried by the callback `<EventStream>` inside
    each sub-MPD, at the times that stream declares on the ad's own
    presentation timeline.
11. At the end of the alternative presentation the Player resumes the primary
    timeline per the event's semantics. Insertion resumes where the main
    timeline paused. Replacement resumes at the position `@returnOffset`
    determines, because under replacement *"the main Media Presentation is not
    being output, but its media time progresses at the same speed as the
    currently playing alternative Media Presentation"* (DASH §5.16.1).

### 6.2 The non-linear flow

An overlay slot composites an ad onto the screen while the primary content keeps
playing underneath. The trigger, the timing arithmetic and the fallback
behaviour are those of §6.1; four things differ.

1. The opportunity is an `<Event>` of the overlay scheme carrying an
   `<svta:OverlayPresentation>` child, which declares `@uri`, `@maxDuration`,
   the `@allowedLayouts` token list, and the earliest-resolution offset.
2. The primary content **keeps playing** for the whole slot. The Player composes
   the ad form onto the primary surface; the primary timeline advances
   throughout, and no return offset arises.
3. The APS answers with an **Overlay Resolution Document**: a static MPD of the
   non-linear resolution profile carrying a single `<Period>` whose only child
   is `<svta:OverlayList>`, holding zero or more `<svta:Candidate>` elements.
   Each candidate declares its duration and carries its presentation options as
   an ordered list of `<svta:RenderableAsset>` elements, its own tracking
   `<EventStream>`, and its own click carrier.
4. The Player **selects one presentation option per candidate**. It walks the
   candidate's options in document order and renders the first whose form its
   device can render and whose `@layout` token appears in the window's
   `@allowedLayouts`. Document order is the preference order, following the base
   specification's own convention for ordered alternatives: *"If multiple URLs
   are provided, the content author expresses the preferences of using one of
   those by the order with the first one having the highest preference"* (DASH
   §5.11.3). A candidate no option satisfies is skipped and the Player moves to
   the next candidate.
5. The candidates that survive are presented **in sequence**, one form active at
   a time, each starting when the previous one ends. Once the candidates are
   exhausted, or the cap is reached, the primary content continues alone.

### 6.3 The pause flow

A pause slot is triggered by the **viewer**, not by the playhead. The
`<svta:PauseAdPresentation>` window declares a region of the primary timeline in
which a viewer pause triggers a resolution request; a pause that begins outside
every such region triggers none.

**When the Player resolves.** Two timings are admissible and they trade
differently.

- **Speculatively, at the Earliest Resolution Time** — the document is in hand
  before the viewer pauses, so the ad appears the instant the paused frame does.
  The cost is a request, and a decision, spent on a pause that may never happen,
  and a decision that ages between the request and the pause.
- **Lazily, at the moment of pause** — no request is spent on a pause that does
  not occur, and the decision is fresh. The cost is the round trip, which the
  viewer spends looking at a paused frame with no ad on it.

**What bounds what.** The window bounds *where* on the timeline a pause
qualifies. The viewer bounds *how long* the resulting slot lasts, which is
unknowable when the MPD is authored. The window's `@maxDuration` bounds the
display of **one** pause ad before automatic dismissal, and bounds neither the
slot nor the pause.

**When the candidates run out.** A pause can outlast the document that fills it.
`<svta:OverlayList>@onCandidatesExhausted` declares what the Player then does:
`repeat` presents the sequence again from the start for as long as the pause
lasts, `request-again` obtains a new resolution document for the same pause, and
`stop` presents no further ad and shows the paused primary frame. Absent the
declaration the behaviour is `stop`. Under `request-again`, a document carrying
no candidates is `stop` for the remainder of that pause.

**Live content.** While the viewer is paused inside a pause window, the Player's
presentation time stays frozen inside that window, even though the live edge
keeps advancing in wall-clock time — which is what keeps the window admissible
for the whole pause. That freeze is bounded by the time-shift buffer: where the
pause outlives the buffer, the resumption point is clipped to the buffer edge,
following the base specification's own rule of *"clipping RT to the timeshift
buffer (i.e. the time interval between TSBS and PHPLE)"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.5). A
Player that then resumes at the live edge does so as an action after the resume,
outside the pause window.

**Dismissal.** The pause ad exists only while playback is paused. On resume the
Player dismisses it and returns to the primary content immediately, including
mid-presentation, and the beacons scheduled after that transition fall outside
the ad's active window.

### 6.4 The resolution request

The resolution request is one HTTP `GET`. Three things compose its URL.

- **The event URL** — the `@uri` declared on the opportunity element, which
  resolves to the APS.
- **The Publisher's query template** — carried by a `<RequestParam>` element
  under the URL-parameter scheme. The base specification defines the template as
  the content author's: it *"provides URL parameters template information. This
  string shall contain one or more `$<ParamIdentifier>$` template identifiers"*
  (DASH §I.2.2.2). The Player substitutes the identifiers with live values and
  appends the result. `@includeInRequests="altmpd"` scopes a template to
  alternative-MPD requests; the non-linear resolution request is named in the
  same attribute by `urn:svta:dash:request:sgai-resolution:2026`, which the base
  specification admits as *"a URN or tag URI, where the request type semantics is
  understood by the client and specified by the URN / tag URI owner"*, and whose
  companion rule — *"The client shall drop unknown URIs from the
  `@includeInRequests` and `@includeInHeaders` strings prior to processing
  them"* (DASH Table I.4) — is what makes the new URI safe in front of a Player
  that has never heard of it.
- **The reserved capability parameters** — `sgaiVideoDecoders`,
  `sgaiImageOverlay` and `sgaiHtmlOverlay`, which the Player attaches to declare
  what its device can render. Each is optional and each is omitted rather than
  sent empty when the Player cannot or will not populate it; an absent parameter
  means undetermined. A Player that attaches a parameter of its own gives it a
  vendor prefix of the form `x-<vendor>-`.

The syntax, the types and the value spaces are in §5.8; this section describes
what the request is made of, not how each part is written.

### 6.5 Interface contracts

| Source | Target | Transport | Format | Direction | Error semantics |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Player | Publisher CDN | HTTP/HTTPS | DASH MPD (XML) | request / response (pull) | HTTP status; a failure to obtain the main MPD is a session-level condition of the base specification and yields no ad opportunity. |
| Player | Publisher CDN | HTTP/HTTPS | media segments | request / response (pull) | HTTP status; segment-level retry per the Player's own policy. |
| Player | APS | HTTP/HTTPS | request: URL + query (§6.4); response: `ListMPD`, single-period alternative MPD, or Overlay Resolution Document (XML) | request / response (pull, synchronous) | Five outcomes are failed executions and are alike: no response or a transport failure; a final status other than `200`; a `200` whose body does not parse; a `200` carrying a well-formed document of the wrong family; and a `200` carrying a well-formed document with no candidates. Each sends the Player to the next window of the family (§7.8). |
| Player | Ad CDN | HTTP/HTTPS | sub-MPD, media segments, image and HTML creatives | request / response (pull) | HTTP status; a failure while rendering an accepted ad aborts that ad and leaves the primary content untouched (§7.10). |
| Player | Tracking endpoints | HTTP/HTTPS | callback beacon (HTTP `GET`) | fire-and-forget | The response is discarded: the base specification states that *"the client is expected to entirely ignore the response"* (DASH §5.10.4.5.1). A beacon failure is non-fatal and reaches neither the ad nor the viewer. |
| APS | ADS | — | — | — | **Outside this specification.** What this specification observes is the document the APS returns; how it was obtained, in what format, and how the APS reacts to an ADS error are bilateral matters between those two actors. |

Transport is HTTPS in production. Authentication, DRM and token exchange layer
on top of it and are outside this specification.

### 6.6 Converting a decision document into a resolution document

**This section is non-normative.** It imposes no obligation on any actor.

This specification depends on no version of VAST, and on no particular decision
format: the ADS is free to emit another one, and an APS that consumes it is
equally conformant. The mapping below is **illustrative**, and it is here
because a VAST-shaped decision document is what most deployments actually hand
the APS, so an implementer reading only the normative chapters would have to
reinvent the correspondence.

| Decision-document field | Resolution-document target |
|---|---|
| Inline ad entry | One `<Period>` carrying an `<ImportedMPD>` in the `ListMPD` (linear), or one `<svta:Candidate>` (non-linear). |
| Wrapper / redirect entry | Resolved inside the APS; nothing of it reaches the Player. |
| Declared duration of the creative | `<Period>@duration` on the `ListMPD` Period and on the sub-MPD Period (linear), or `<svta:Candidate>@duration` (non-linear). |
| Media file, one per encoding | One `<Representation>` inside an `<AdaptationSet>` of the sub-MPD; several collapse into one adaptive ladder. Bitrate, width, height and codec map onto the corresponding `<Representation>` attributes, and the base specification binds the type: *"The @mimeType attribute of each Representation shall be provided according to IETF RFC 4337"* (DASH §7.3.1). |
| Non-AV creative (image, HTML) | `<svta:RenderableAsset>@assetUrl`, with `@form` set to `image` or `html`. |
| Tracking events (start, quartiles, complete, and the rest) | `<Event>` entries in the callback `<EventStream>`, at the matching times on the ad's own presentation timeline. |
| Impression | A callback `<Event>` at presentation time `0`. |
| Click-through URL | `<svta:Click>@clickThroughUrl`. |
| Click-tracking URLs | `<svta:ClickTracking>` children of that same `<svta:Click>`, fired on activation rather than at a presentation time. |
| Ad system, ad title, advertiser | `<svta:AdSystem>`, `<svta:AdTitle>`, `<svta:Advertiser>`. Optional to emit and optional to read. |
| Universal ad identifier | Stays on the decisioning side, where reconciliation happens. An APS with a reason to propagate it has the optional `<svta:UniversalAdId>` element, which a Player is free to ignore. |
| Error entry | No carrier. What the Player observes is an opportunity that yields no ad. |

Four cases are worth calling out, because they are where the transformation
stops being mechanical.

- **Ad pods.** A decision carrying several ads becomes several `<Period>`
  elements in the `ListMPD`, or several `<svta:Candidate>` elements in the
  Overlay Resolution Document, in the order the decision declared. The slot cap
  applies to the pod as a whole, and the Player is what enforces it.
- **Wrapper chains.** The APS resolves redirects until it reaches inline
  creatives, before the Player sees anything. The Player has no visibility into
  the hops, which is what keeps the Player-to-APS interface at one request per
  slot.
- **A tracking-only decision entry, with no media.** The APS has nothing to
  build a renderable option from. This specification declines to bind the APS's
  policy here — whether it omits the entry, or reports an error upstream, is a
  matter between the APS and the ADS. What the Player observes is fixed either
  way: a document with one candidate fewer, and, where that leaves no candidates
  at all, the empty resolution below.
- **The no-fill response.** A decision carrying no ads converts into a
  resolution document **carrying no candidates**, served `200` with a body: an
  `<svta:OverlayList>` with no children for the non-linear families, and for the
  linear family a `ListMPD` whose merge yields no available media. A no-fill is
  a legitimate decision rather than an error, and expressing it as a document
  rather than as an error status is what lets the Player tell it apart from a
  broken APS.

## 7. Expected behaviour

### 7.1 How to read this chapter

This chapter states the behaviour a conformant implementation exhibits, per
actor and per scenario. Each section names the annex that walks it through end
to end with manifests. Three things hold across every row.

**Declining the opportunity is a defined outcome.** Where no candidate carries
an option the device can render, or the window's declarations admit none of the
options offered, the Player declines the slot and the primary content continues.
That is the specified result, and an implementation reaching it behaved
correctly.

**The primary content's continuity is the invariant.** Primary-content playback
survives every row below: no freeze, no blank slate, no error surface unless the
application asked for one, and no beacon for an opportunity that yielded no ad.
A viewer cannot tell from the screen that an opportunity existed and went
unfilled.

**Device class changes some outcomes and not others.** Where it does, behaviour
is stated per class using the decoder and surface budgets of §3.4; where it does
not, this chapter says so once instead of repeating five identical rows.

### 7.2 Linear ad break

A linear ad takes over the primary content surface. Pre-roll, mid-roll and the
multi-ad break differ in where the window sits and in how many ads the decision
carries; they do not differ in Player behaviour, and **the device class does not
change the outcome for any of them** — a linear ad occupies the one decoder the
primary content was using, so D1 to D5 play the break alike.

- The Publisher MUST declare the slot's cap on the opportunity element, and MUST
  place all windows of one family in one `<EventStream>` per `<Period>`.
- The Player MUST resolve the window at a randomised instant between the
  Earliest Resolution Time and the event's `@presentationTime`, MUST play the
  ads in the order the document declares, and MUST resume the primary timeline
  per the event's semantics when the break ends.
- The Player MUST enforce the cap. On a replacement slot the cap bounds *until
  when*: with `@clip` at its default the presentation ends at the scheduled end,
  so a late start yields a shorter ad, and with `@clip="false"` it ends one
  cap-length after the actual start. On an insertion slot it bounds *how long*:
  the cumulative rendered length of the break.
- The Player MUST convert a candidate's declared duration into the cap's
  timescale, rounding up to the next whole unit, before comparing; a converted
  duration equal to the cap is admitted. It MAY drop a candidate before playback
  whose declared duration would push the cumulative length past the cap, and
  MUST trim at the cap boundary a candidate it accepted whose actual length
  exceeds it, the beacons scheduled after that boundary stopping there.
- A slot whose declaration carries **no** cap yields no ads: the Player MUST
  continue with the primary content. This narrows the base specification, which
  reads an absent cap as infinity, and is **provisional and open with the working
  group**. A declared cap of zero means the opportunity does not fire.
- The ADS MAY return a decision whose total length exceeds the cap; the cap is
  enforced downstream, by the Player.

Annex A walks the pre-roll, Annex B the mid-roll, and Annex F the multi-ad
break.

### 7.3 Non-linear overlay

An overlay is composited on the primary content, which keeps playing. The Player
renders the first presentation option a candidate declares that its device can
satisfy and the window's `@allowedLayouts` admits, so the outcome varies by class
with the decoder-and-surface budget of §3.4.

| Class | What the Player renders | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **D1** | Any offered form: `video`, `image` or `html`, in any overlay or squeezeback layout the window admits. | Two or more decoders, plus an image surface and an HTML surface over video. |
| **D2** | A `video` overlay or `video` squeezeback. An `image` or `html` option is declined and the Player MUST continue down the option list. | A second decoder exists, and no non-video surface composites over video. A `squeezeback-double-box-with-background` needs an image surface for the background and is declined whatever the ad's form. |
| **D3** | An `image` or `html` overlay, or the corresponding squeezeback. A `video` option is declined. | One decoder, which the primary content holds; image and HTML surfaces both exist. |
| **D4** | An `image` overlay or `image` squeezeback. `video` and `html` options are declined. | One decoder and an image surface; no HTML over video. |
| **D5** | No overlay layout. The Player renders only a `linear` full-screen takeover, and only where the window's `@allowedLayouts` admits that token. | One decoder and no overlay surface of any kind. |

- The Player MUST walk a candidate's options in document order and render the
  first that satisfies both its device and the window's `@allowedLayouts`. It
  MUST skip a candidate no option satisfies, move to the next, and continue with
  the primary content alone once the candidates are exhausted. A skip is not a
  failed execution: the Player stays inside the document it obtained.
- The APS MUST carry the options as an ordered list in preference order; a
  candidate carrying exactly one option is equally conformant and places the
  suitability call with the APS.
- The `linear` layout token is satisfiable on every class and is the option of
  last resort on a non-linear slot: it monetises D5 at the cost of the screen.

Annex C walks a coexisting overlay across the classes, Annex I the ordered
option list resolved on each of them, Annex J the three-element side-by-side
layout, and Annex M a slot where the Player declared its capabilities and the APS
returned one option.

### 7.4 Coexisting and sequenced forms in one slot

- A non-linear slot MAY be filled by several candidates, and the Player MUST
  present them **in sequence**, in the order the resolution document declares,
  each starting when the previous one ends.
- At any instant **at most one** non-linear ad form is active on the screen. The
  reason is a budget: one active form means a device needs the primary content
  plus one ad form, which is exactly what §3.4 assigns to each class. A second
  concurrent form would demand a decoder or a surface no class is guaranteed to
  have, and the slot would then resolve differently on hardware the Publisher
  cannot see.
- The Player MUST enforce the slot cap against the sequence's cumulative length.
- The APS MUST declare several forms as a sequence of candidates. No construct
  here expresses concurrent composition of two non-linear forms, which leaves
  the single-active-form bound with nothing to author around it.

Annex C walks this, with several candidates and several layouts in one window.

### 7.5 Hybrid: a linear ad with a concurrent overlay

A linear break and an overlay window can be active over the same stretch of the
timeline. The linear ad is the primary surface and the overlay composites on top
of it, so the budget is that of §7.3 with the linear ad in the place of the
primary content.

| Class | Outcome |
|---|---|
| **D1** | The linear ad plays and the overlay is composited over it, in any offered form. |
| **D2** | The linear ad plays; the overlay is rendered only where it offers a `video` option. Otherwise the linear ad plays alone. |
| **D3** | The linear ad plays with an `image` or `html` overlay on top. |
| **D4** | The linear ad plays with an `image` overlay on top; an `html` option is declined. |
| **D5** | The linear ad plays alone; the overlay opportunity is declined. |

Each window binds the ad it serves with its own cap and allowed layouts. Where
the overlay is declined, the Player MUST leave the linear break untouched: one
declined opportunity affects no other.

Annex D walks this, and Annex N the related case where what the overlay
composites over is a replacement that is not advertising.

### 7.6 Pause-triggered ad

The Player MUST request a resolution document when a viewer pause begins inside
a pause window, which marks where a pause qualifies rather than a schedule.

| Class | Outcome |
|---|---|
| **D1** | Any form, on either surface. |
| **D2** | `video` fullscreen, rendered on either decoder with the primary content's frame released. `image` and `html` are declined on both surfaces, the class carrying no non-video surface (§5.3.7.3). Partial: `video` only. <!-- refine: v9-spec-validation.md#T1 --> |
| **D3** | Any form fullscreen; `image` or `html` partial, a `video` partial needing a second decoder the class lacks. |
| **D4** | `video` or `image` fullscreen; `html` is declined, the class carrying no HTML surface (§5.3.7.3). `image` partial. <!-- refine: v9-spec-validation.md#T1 --> |
| **D5** | A `video` pause ad on the `pause-fullscreen` surface, which the Player MAY render by releasing the primary content's resources. The class carries no image or HTML surface and no partial surface, so the other combinations are declined. |

Lifecycle obligations, which do not vary by class:

- On the pause-to-play transition the Player MUST remove a rendered pause ad
  from the screen **within one rendering frame**, MUST return to the primary
  content immediately including mid-presentation, and MUST stop firing that ad's
  beacons; beacons scheduled later fall outside the ad's active window.
- In live content the Player MUST keep its presentation time frozen inside the
  pause window for the duration of the pause, up to the time-shift buffer: where
  the pause outlives the buffer, it MUST clip the resumption point to the buffer
  edge. Resuming at the live edge is a Player action after the resume, outside
  the pause window.
- Where the window is declared once-per-session, the Player MUST present at most
  one pause ad for it and MUST leave a later qualifying pause uninterrupted. The
  window is consumed when a pause ad **begins rendering**, so a pause resolving
  to no renderable candidate leaves it available — the base specification's own
  accounting: *"counter E.c has not been incremented due to the failure,
  consequently if E.c = 0 the event can still be executed in the future"* (DASH
  §5.16.2.2.6 NOTE 3).
- Where the candidates run out mid-pause, the Player MUST apply the behaviour
  the document declares, and `stop` where it declares none (§6.3).

Annex E walks the pause-triggered ad end to end.

### 7.7 Cross-family priority

While the viewer is paused inside a pause window, the pause ad is the only ad
surface visible.

- Where an overlay runs, the Player MUST render the pause ad and suspend
  the overlay, fullscreen or partial alike.
- Where a linear ad occupies the screen, the Player MUST present the pause ad
  and suspend the linear ad, resuming it from where it stopped.
- On resume the Player MUST dismiss the pause ad within one rendering frame,
  stop its remaining beacons, and restore the suspended form **only where its
  window is still open**. Where that window expired during the pause, the Player
  MUST leave the surface clear and continue with the primary content.
- Cap arithmetic runs on the presentation timeline, which does not advance during
  a pause, so a suspended form resumes with the cap it had.
- This priority is fixed: this specification carries no construct through which
  the Publisher, the ADS or the APS inverts it.

Annex H walks an overlay window crossing a pause window.

### 7.8 Overlapping windows of one family

Two or more windows of one family overlapping in time are a declared **fallback
chain**, not a concurrency case. The Player MUST order them by presentation
time, oldest first, breaking ties by position inside the `<EventStream>`, and
MUST resolve the first.
- An attempt that produces no ad is a **failed execution**, and the Player MUST
  attempt the next window of the family. Five conditions are failed executions
  and are alike: no response or a transport failure; a final HTTP status other
  than `200`; a `200` whose body does not parse; a `200` carrying a well-formed
  document with no candidates; and a `200` carrying a well-formed document of
  the **wrong family**.
- The wrong-family document is a failure to resolve, so it **continues** the
  chain. Reading it as an empty resolution would end the chain instead, and one
  misrouted response would silence every remaining window of the family — two
  opposite outcomes from the same HTTP status.
- A document that **carries candidates** is not a failed execution, whatever the
  Player does with them: a slot whose candidates all proved unrenderable ends
  with the primary content, and the chain is not resumed.
- Each window binds the candidates it serves with **its own** allowed layouts
  and cap, inheriting none from the window it stands in for. This is a Player
  obligation.
- Where every window has been attempted, the primary content continues
  uninterrupted — the base specification's own floor: *"A failed execution
  results in smooth continued playback of the main media presentation"* (DASH
  §5.16.2.2.6), and *"If no event can be successfully executed, the playback
  continues uninterrupted"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.5).

Annex L walks an overlapping pair with the fallback exercised.

### 7.9 A Player that predates this specification

Every construct this specification introduces is foreign-namespace open content
in an MPD the base specification already requires to survive its removal: *"the
MPD shall be authored such that, after XML attributes or elements in the other
namespaces than the DASH namespace are removed, the result is a valid XML
document"* (DASH §5.2.1).

Such a Player therefore skips the overlay window, the pause window and every
element they carry, never reaches the APS, and plays the primary content
uninterrupted. The single invariant asserted here is that **the new construct is
skipped silently** — no error, no freeze, no artefact — and nothing beyond it,
because the base specification specifies no client behaviour normatively: *"as
DASH Client operation is not specified normatively in this document, it is also
unspecified how a DASH Client conforms to a particular profile"* (DASH §8.1
NOTE 1). What the viewer sees then depends on what the **Publisher** authored,
and that choice is content-dependent.

- **On live content**, the opportunity is an expected loss and the primary
  content continues. Holding a live stream to splice a break costs real content,
  so letting the older Player keep playing the live edge is what the Publisher
  SHOULD plan for.
- **On on-demand content**, the Publisher MAY author a standard linear break
  from baseline constructs alongside the new construct. A Player predating this
  specification skips what it does not recognise and plays that break, so the
  opportunity is monetised rather than lost, while a Player implementing this
  specification takes the richer path. Since the Publisher cannot detect a
  Player version from the manifest, the fallback is authored unconditionally.

One authoring consequence follows. A baseline element nested **inside** an SGAI
element carries no legacy guarantee, because removing the foreign-namespace
parent removes the child with it — which is why the tracking `<EventStream>`
inside a candidate is authored for Players implementing this specification. A
click is the same case: a conformant Player MUST read `<svta:Click>` and fire
its click-tracking on activation, and no construct in any namespace reaches a
Player that has never heard of this specification.

Annex G walks this, and Annex K walks the ClickThrough.

### 7.10 Runtime failure during an accepted ad

Where resolving or rendering an ad the Player already accepted fails — a decode
error, a malformed candidate, a segment returning an error status, a network
loss mid-ad — the Player MUST abort that ad and continue the primary content
uninterrupted, with no visible artefact. It MAY skip to the next candidate in
document order, MAY end the break, and MAY retry the failed segment first; which
it does is its own policy. It fires no beacon for an opportunity that failed
this way, and MAY surface an error to the viewer only where the application
opted in. A beacon failure is non-fatal throughout: it affects neither the ad
nor the primary content.

### 7.11 Playback speed

The Player MUST render every ad form, linear and non-linear alike, at the
primary content's playback speed at the moment the ad is presented, following
that speed where it differs from 1×.

A form's declared duration is a value on the **presentation timeline**, for
every form including `image` and `html`, which carry no intrinsic media. The
Player MUST derive the wall-clock length on screen as `duration /
playback_speed` — a 10-second form at 2× occupies 5 seconds — so that length is
derived at presentation rather than authored. Cap enforcement and beacon
scheduling run on the presentation timeline, which is what keeps both meaningful
at any speed.

## 8. Implementation notes

### 8.1 What this chapter is

This chapter is **non-normative**. It explains how to handle the conditions a
deployment meets, and where it names an obligation it points at the chapter that
carries it — §4, §5, §6, §7. Nothing here creates one.

It exists to make one distinction operationally clear. An **unfilled
opportunity** — a document that parses, belongs to the family that asked, and
declares that nothing was sold — is a commercial outcome; a **failed
resolution** — no usable document at all — is a delivery fault. Both end with
uninterrupted primary content, so the screen cannot tell them apart, and only
the first is expected traffic. The **misrouted document** is the harder case: a
well-formed resolution of the wrong family looks like a served response on every
hop, fills nothing, and cannot be diagnosed from the viewer's screen at all.

### 8.2 The conditions, and what to do about each

| Condition | What the Player does | What it MAY do | What the other actors owe |
|---|---|---|---|
| The resolution request fails at transport level — name resolution, refused connection, TLS failure, timeout before any final status — or returns a final HTTP status other than `200`, including an APS that declines to answer because a capability parameter was absent | Treats the attempt as a failed execution, attempts the next window of the same family, and continues with the primary content once the family is exhausted (§7) | Re-attempt inside the interval between the earliest resolution time and the window's presentation time; surface the failure through an implementation-defined API | Publisher: declare a fallback window where continuity matters, an earliest-resolution offset wide enough to leave room before the slot, and all windows of one family in one event stream per period. APS: answer whatever subset of capability parameters arrives, including none (§5.8) |
| The response is `200` but the body does not parse as a resolution document — malformed XML, unknown root, schema-invalid content | Treats the attempt as a failed execution, renders nothing from that document, and continues down the fallback chain | Log the parse or validation failure | APS: emit a document valid against the base schema plus the extension points this specification uses. A validation pass that reports such a document valid while skipping its foreign-namespace subtree has not checked the tracking carrier at all |
| The response is `200` and carries a well-formed resolution document of the **wrong family** | Treats it as a failure to resolve and continues down the fallback chain, rather than as a resolution carrying no candidates | Report the family mismatch through an implementation-defined API | APS: answer the slot that asked. A document of the wrong family fills nothing whatever it contains, and reading it as an empty resolution would let one misrouted response silence every remaining window |
| The response is `200`, the document is well-formed and complete, and it **carries no candidates** | Treats the attempt as a failed execution and attempts the next window of the family; leaves a once-per-session window still executable, since no presentation started | Report the opportunity as unfilled rather than failed | APS: express a no-ads decision as a document carrying no candidates, served `200` with a body (§5.2). ADS: nothing — no-fill is a legitimate decision |
| The resolution document arrives after the window has elapsed | Keeps the slot inside what the cap bounds for that family and keeps the primary content uninterrupted; on an inherited linear replacement slot a late start shortens the presentation rather than moving its scheduled end | Discard the document (§8.5) | Publisher: declare an earliest-resolution offset that gives the APS a usable head start. APS: answer inside the window it was asked in; its latency budget toward the ADS is bilateral and outside this specification |
| The slot declaration carries no maximum duration, or declares a maximum of zero | With no maximum declared, presents no ads from that slot and continues with the primary content; with a maximum of zero, the opportunity does not fire | Report the defective declaration through an implementation-defined API | Publisher: declare a maximum duration on every slot, linear and non-linear. The treatment of an absent maximum is provisional (§8.12) |
| No presentation option on a candidate is satisfiable — the device renders none of the offered forms, or every offered layout needs more concurrent decoders or compositing surfaces than the device has (§3.4) | Skips that candidate, moves to the next in document order, and reaches the primary content only once every candidate is exhausted; stays on this document, because a document carrying candidates is not a failed execution | Report the skip through an implementation-defined API | APS: carry the presentation options as an ordered list whose document order is the preference order (§5.3). A candidate carrying a single option places the suitability call upstream (§8.7) |
| A presentation option names a layout the window does not allow, or one outside the layout enumeration (§3.2) | Moves to the next option in document order, and skips the candidate when none passes both the device check and the allowed-layouts check; checks against **the window that served the candidate**, which binds it with its own allowed layouts and its own cap | Report the rejected layout name | Publisher: draw allowed-layout names from the enumeration, and name a pause surface on a pause window. APS: emit form metadata only for the enumerated ad types and placements |
| A candidate's creative carrier sits outside the admissible set of video, image and HTML (§3.3), or a non-audiovisual asset URL is expressed on a path where the base specification binds the media type to the RFC 4337 registry | Renders only forms its device can render | Skip the candidate as a non-conformant upstream signal and fall through as above | APS and Publisher: every creative carries a media type inside the admissible set, and a non-audiovisual asset URL travels on one of the carriers §5.3 defines for it |
| The slot's cap is reached — a candidate's **declared** duration would push the cumulative total past it, or an accepted candidate's **actual** rendered length runs past its declared duration | Where the cap bounds cumulative length, stops rendering once the cumulative total would exceed it, even mid-ad, enforcing against actual length; stops the remaining beacons at the trim boundary; keeps the slot inside what the cap bounds whatever the ADS metadata or candidate count say; converts a candidate's duration into the cap's timescale, rounding up, and admits a converted duration equal to the cap; keeps the surviving candidates in document order | Drop a candidate before playback on declared duration alone; surface the trim through an implementation-defined API | Publisher: declare the cap on every slot. ADS: free to select without regard to the cap — a conformance check passes on cumulative overflow alone. Cap arithmetic runs on the presentation timeline, so a form suspended during a pause resumes with the cap it had |
| Rendering an accepted candidate fails at runtime — an ad segment returns an error status, a decode failure, a mid-ad network loss | Aborts that ad and continues the primary content uninterrupted (§8.6) | Move to the next candidate in document order, or end the break; retry the ad segment before aborting | APS: reference ad media reachable for the duration of the slot |
| An event scheme, extension element or foreign namespace is unknown to the Player, in the main MPD or in the resolution document | Ignores the unknown construct with its whole subtree and keeps playing the primary content | Log the unknown scheme or namespace; ignore the application-level metadata elements entirely, which are optional to emit and optional to read | Publisher and APS: express every construct through an extension point whose ignore-if-unknown semantics produce this behaviour on a Player that predates this specification, leaving pre-existing semantics unchanged |
| A tracking beacon or a click-tracking request fails, or a beacon falls at a moment the ad never reaches — past a trim boundary, or after a pause ad was dismissed on resume | Leaves the ad and the primary content unaffected; stops the remaining beacons at a trim boundary and from the pause-to-play transition onward; fires each beacon once per candidate that carries it, treating the same identifier in two candidates as two beacons | Retry the beacon and log the failure; report the unfired beacons | APS: carry beacons as events of the callback scheme timed on the candidate's own presentation, and the ClickThrough with its click-tracking in the carrier §5.6 defines. ADS: owns which beacons exist and when |
| The resolution document implies two non-linear forms on screen at one instant, or a pause begins inside a pause window while an overlay or a linear ad occupies the screen | Keeps at most one non-linear form active: presents sequenced forms one after another in declared order; during a pause suspends an overlay and renders the pause ad whatever its surface, suspends a linear ad and resumes it from where it stopped, and restores a suspended form on resume only while its window is still open | Release the resources held by the primary content and by a pre-existing overlay to present a full-screen pause ad; report the suspended form | APS: declare forms as a sequence rather than a concurrent composition. No construct in any namespace inverts the pause-ad-over-overlay priority |
| The pause-ad candidates are exhausted while the viewer is still paused, or the pause window was already consumed | Applies the exhaustion behaviour the resolution document declares, and `stop` when it declares none; under `request-again`, treats a document carrying no candidates as `stop` for the rest of that pause; returns to the primary content immediately on resume, even mid-presentation; on a once-per-session window presents at most one pause ad and leaves a later qualifying pause uninterrupted, counting the window consumed when a pause ad begins rendering | Report the applied behaviour through an implementation-defined API | APS: declare the exhaustion behaviour on every pause resolution document — that declaration is the APS's, not the Publisher's. Publisher: declare the once-per-session bound where wanted, and request the pause-delivery metric (§5.9) |

Three of these rows carry more than the table can hold.

**Two fall-throughs, not one.** *Window-level* fall-through — transport failure,
unparseable body, wrong family, no candidates — advances to the next window of
the family and reaches the primary content once the family is exhausted.
*Candidate-level* fall-through — no satisfiable option, a disallowed layout, an
inadmissible carrier — advances to the next candidate inside the document already
in hand and never to the next window, because a document carrying candidates
resolved successfully. Conflating the two either wastes fallback windows on a
document that answered, or abandons one that had a usable candidate further
down.

**The unfilled opportunity leaves a once-per-session window intact**, because
nothing was presented. The base specification's own execution counter counts the
*"number of times alternative MPD playback successfully started"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.2),
and it records that a failed execution leaves that counter untouched, so an
event declared once-per-session stays executable (DASH §5.16.2.2.6 NOTE 3).

**A beacon's response carries nothing.** The callback carrier is fire-and-forget
— *"the client is expected to entirely ignore the response"* (DASH §5.10.4.5.1) — so
a tracking failure is diagnosable only on the tracking endpoint's side.

### 8.3 Order of precedence

When several conditions arise on one exchange, they apply in this order:

1. **Transport.** No document arrived, so nothing downstream applies.
2. **Resolution-document level** — unusable, misrouted, empty, or late. All but
   the late document put the Player on the fallback chain.
3. **The window's own declarations**, validated before anything is rendered: the
   cap, the allowed layouts, the admissible carriers, and a drop-before-play on
   declared duration. A slot with no declared cap precedes all of these.
4. **Per-candidate decode-time** — what the device can satisfy, and what the ad
   CDN delivers.
5. **Per-candidate playback-time** — the cap against actual length, the
   single-active-form bound, exhaustion inside a pause.
6. **Tracking failures**, non-fatal throughout: never aborting an ad, never
   reaching the viewer.

An unknown construct is orthogonal to this order: it is ignored wherever it
appears, at any level, and ignoring it never advances the Player to the next
step — the presence of something unrecognised fails no window and skips no
candidate.

### 8.4 Tracking-only decision entries

An ad decision entry may carry tracking and no media: the ADS wants the beacons
fired but offers no creative. The APS cannot synthesise a candidate with nothing
to render, so the entry either disappears from the resolution document or is
reported back to the ADS as an error. This specification declines to bind that
policy — the choice sits on the APS-to-ADS interface, outside its scope. What the
Player observes is the same either way: a document with one candidate fewer, or,
when every entry was tracking-only, a document carrying no candidates, which is
the unfilled resolution of §8.2 and puts the Player on the fallback chain.

The industry convention between silent omission and a signalled error has no
answer in the base specification, and this specification invents none. An APS
operator choosing one should record which, since the two produce identical
Player-side behaviour and different reporting on the ADS side.

### 8.5 Late callbacks and late documents

A resolution document can land after the window it answers has elapsed, and a
beacon can fall at a moment the ad never reaches. **What the cap bounds differs
by family.** On a linear replacement slot it
bounds *until when*: with clipping left at its default the presentation ends at
the scheduled end, so a late start shortens the presentation rather than moving
that end. On a linear insertion slot and on every non-linear family it bounds
*how long* — the cumulative rendered length, which a late start does not change.
On a pause slot it bounds one pause ad's display, the slot's own length being
set by the viewer.

**Beacons stop where the presentation stops** — at a trim boundary, and from a
pause ad's dismissal onward. A beacon scheduled past either point is not fired
late; it is not fired at all.

**No retry count, backoff or deadline for the resolution request is fixed by
this specification.** An implementer choosing one should weigh: the room
available is the interval between the earliest resolution time and the window's
presentation time, so a retry answered after it buys nothing and a deadline
there costs nothing; each retry spends an APS request that may never be used;
and the APS's latency toward the ADS sits inside the same budget, invisible to
the Player.

### 8.6 Error recovery without a visible artefact

"The primary content continues uninterrupted" is a property of the screen, not a
figure of speech. Operationally: no freeze, no blank slate, no error overlay
unless the application explicitly opted in, no beacon fired for the opportunity
that failed, and primary-content playback continuing on its own timeline. The
viewer is unable to tell that an ad opportunity existed. This is the base
specification's own floor for its execution model: *"A failed execution results
in smooth continued playback of the main media presentation"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.6),
and, once every window has been tried, *"If no event can be successfully
executed, the playback continues uninterrupted"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.5).

One boundary here is normative rather than a matter of API shape. An error
overlay is a visible artefact, so rendering one on any of the conditions in §8.2
breaks the guarantee §7 states, unless the application explicitly opted in.
Everything else in this chapter about surfacing a condition is guidance; this
one is not.

### 8.7 Device-class fallbacks

An APS that receives no capability parameters still serves every device class by
carrying several presentation options on the candidate, ordered from the most
demanding to the least and ending in the `linear` full-screen takeover — the only
layout satisfiable on all five classes, and so the option of last resort on a
non-linear slot. The Player renders the first option its device and the window
both admit, so each class lands on the richest option it can run.

An APS that does receive them may instead emit a single option chosen for the
device that asked. Responsibility for that option's suitability then sits
upstream, with the APS or the ADS that returned one option to it: if it is
unsatisfiable, the Player skips the whole candidate.

**The Player-visible interface is identical in the two cases**, and nothing in
the document distinguishes a candidate whose options were filtered from one that
never had more. That is acceptable because the Player's rule does not depend on
the difference — it renders the first satisfiable option and skips the candidate
when none passes — so no behaviour is lost, and a marker would add a field only
diagnostics read. What it costs is diagnosis: a skipped candidate looks the same
whether the APS narrowed against wrong capability data or never had a second
option, and only APS-side logs separate the two.

### 8.8 Resolving a pause opportunity

A pause window can be resolved speculatively, at its earliest resolution time, or
lazily, at the moment the viewer pauses. The base specification records the early
attempt as admissible — *"Attempting resolution at time PRTA is an acceptable
practice"* (DASH §5.16.2.2.6 NOTE 2), that time being the earliest resolution time in
its vocabulary — while noting its cost.

**Speculative.** The document is in hand before the pause, so the first frame
appears with no round trip. It costs freshness — targeting, frequency capping
and budget pacing all age between the decision and the pause — and it costs
requests never used, since most pause windows are traversed without a pause.

**Lazy.** The decision is as fresh as it can be and no request is made for a
pause that never happens. The viewer waits the full round trip — Player to APS,
APS to ADS and back — before the first frame, against a paused frame already on
screen. A middle position resolves speculatively where a pause is likely and
re-resolves lazily when the document in hand is stale; no threshold is fixed
here.

### 8.9 The live freeze and the time-shift buffer

In live content the Player's presentation time stays frozen inside the pause
window for as long as the viewer remains paused, which is what keeps the pause ad
admissible while the live edge advances.

**That freeze is bounded by the time-shift buffer.** When a pause outlives the
buffer the base specification clips the resumption time to it — *"clipping RT to
the timeshift buffer (i.e. the time interval between TSBS and PHPLE)"*
(DASH §5.16.2.2.5), the interval named being the buffer between its start and the
live edge. The freeze therefore cannot be promised past the buffer depth.

A clip to the buffer edge, or a jump to the live edge rather than a resume in
place, is still a resume: the pause ends, so the pause ad is dismissed within
one rendering frame and its remaining beacons stop. The repositioning that
follows is a Player action after the resume, outside the pause window, and it
neither extends the pause ad nor revives its beacons.

**Open point.** Whether the pause ad is dismissed *at the buffer boundary* — the
moment the pause outlives the buffer, before the viewer resumes — is not settled
here. The candidate behaviours are to dismiss there and show the paused frame,
or to keep presenting until the viewer acts.

### 8.10 Surfacing conditions to the application layer

This specification defines no event names, no payloads and no delivery mechanism
for any of the conditions in §8.2. A Player that exposes nothing at all to the
application layer is conformant, and the API shape is left to implementers.

What is worth surfacing anyway, in the order of operational value:

- **Unfilled versus failed resolution** — invisible on the screen, and what
  separates a commercial outcome from a delivery fault (§8.1).
- **The misrouted document** — the condition that looks like success on every
  hop and cannot be seen from the viewer's side at all.
- **A candidate skipped for lack of a satisfiable option** — creative supply and
  device population have drifted apart.
- **A trim at the cap** — what was sold exceeded what was declared.
- **Unfired beacons**, which reconcile against the tracking endpoint's counts.

### 8.11 Deriving the pause-delivery measurement

The quantity worth measuring on a pause slot is the fraction of the paused
interval that carried an ad, not the number of opportunities: the count is set
by the viewer, while the filled fraction moves with what the APS returns.

The paused interval is derived from the base specification's play-list metric —
*"A list of playback periods"*, where a playback period is *"the
time interval between a user action and whichever occurs soonest of the next
user action, the end of playback or a failure that stops playback"* (DASH Annex
D.4.6). Two of its fields carry everything a pause slot needs: each entry
declares a start type whose value space includes a resume from pause, and each
rendered stretch a stop reason whose value space includes a user request and
rebuffering.

**The derivation.** The paused interval runs from the end of a playback period
whose last rendered stretch stopped on a **user request** to the start of the
next entry whose start type is a **resume**; against it, the duration the pause
ad occupied is the filled fraction. A period that stopped on **rebuffering** is
a stall rather than a pause opportunity, and the pause window never triggered.

This specification defines no metric of its own for pause-ad delivery and no
transport for reporting one. The base specification takes the same position
about its own metrics: *"This document does not define mechanisms for reporting
metrics; however, it does define a set of metrics and a mechanism that may be
used by the service provider to trigger metric collection and reporting at the
clients, if a reporting mechanism is available"* (DASH §5.9.1). Collection is
triggered through the metric declaration on the main MPD (§5.9), so content
carrying pause windows that omits it leaves the derivation above describing a
quantity nothing obliges anyone to collect.

### 8.12 Open points

What this edition has not settled, so a reader can tell a chosen silence from an
oversight.

- **A slot declaration carrying no maximum duration.** This specification
  presents no ads from such a slot, diverging from the base specification's
  unbounded default — *"If absent, the value is assumed to be infinity, in which
  case the current presentation resumes only when the alternative presentation
  terminates"* (DASH §5.16.5.2). The position is provisional and open with the
  working group, weighing which failure is worse: a Publisher who sells nothing
  from a slot and takes a while to notice, or an ad that runs unbounded.
- **The bound on the live freeze** (§8.9): whether the pause ad is dismissed at
  the time-shift buffer boundary, before the viewer resumes.
- **Whether this specification should publish an interoperability point URI**
  that explicitly includes its constructs. Every construct here is
  foreign-namespace open content, and the base specification requires that the
  document stay valid once such content is removed — *"the MPD shall be authored
  such that, after XML attributes or elements in the other namespaces than the
  DASH namespace are removed, the result is a valid XML document formatted
  according to that schema and that conforms to this document"* (§5.2.1). A
  checker applying that removal before checking a profile validates a document
  from which these constructs have already disappeared. What a published URI
  would buy, against what it costs in profile proliferation, is not decided here.
- **A single-option candidate is indistinguishable from one that never had
  more** (§8.7). No field records that an APS filtered the options, so a skipped
  candidate is diagnosable only from APS-side logs.

# Annexes

<!-- refine: v9-detail-review.md#flag-3 -->
The annexes are informative. Each walks one scenario through the normative
chapters, with the complete documents an implementation would exchange.

## Annex A — Pre-roll (linear)

### A.1 The scenario

A viewer starts a 45-minute on-demand programme. The Publisher has declared one
ad opportunity at the very start of the session, before the first frame of the
primary content. The opportunity is linear: the ad takes over the whole viewport,
and when it finishes the programme begins. The slot is bounded at 30 seconds.

The opportunity is carried by the base specification's alternative-MPD
**insertion** event, which is the construct for a break that adds time to the
session rather than consuming primary content. DASH §5.16.1 describes an
alternative Media Presentation that "may be inserted in the media time of the
Main Presentation, i.e. time-shift the part of the Main Presentation which is
played after the alternative Media Presentation", and states that "After the
playback of the alternative Media Presentation is complete, the playback is
continued with the main Media Presentation."

### A.2 What the Publisher declares

<!-- refine: v9-dash-conformance-audit.md#N1 -->
```xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<MPD xmlns="urn:mpeg:dash:schema:mpd:2011"
     profiles="urn:mpeg:dash:profile:advanced-linear:2025"
     type="static"
     mediaPresentationDuration="PT45M"
     minBufferTime="PT2S"
     publishTime="2026-09-18T12:00:00Z">

  <!-- Query parameters the Player appends to the resolution request (§5.8). -->
  <RequestParam includeInRequests="altmpd"
                queryTemplate="session_id=$urn:mpeg:dash:state:cmcd#sid$"/>

  <Period id="main" start="PT0S">

    <!-- The pre-roll opportunity (§5.1). -->
    <EventStream schemeIdUri="urn:mpeg:dash:event:alternativeMPD:insert:2025"
                 timescale="1000">
      <Event id="101" presentationTime="0" duration="30000">
        <InsertPresentation uri="https://ads.example.com/aps/preroll"
                            maxDuration="30000"
                            earliestResolutionTimeOffset="0"
                            executeOnce="true"/>
      </Event>
    </EventStream>

    <!-- Primary content. -->
    <AdaptationSet id="1" contentType="video"
                   segmentAlignment="true" startWithSAP="1">
      <SegmentTemplate timescale="1000" duration="2000"
                       initialization="https://cdn.example.com/primary/init.mp4"
                       media="https://cdn.example.com/primary/seg_$Number$.m4s"
                       startNumber="1"/>
      <Representation id="v1" mimeType="video/mp4" codecs="avc1.4d401f"
                      bandwidth="2500000" width="1280" height="720"/>
    </AdaptationSet>

  </Period>
</MPD>
```

The cap is `maxDuration="30000"`, in the parent `<EventStream>@timescale` units,
so 30 000 ms. `earliestResolutionTimeOffset="0"` is authored explicitly because
the event sits at presentation time 0 and the default offset of 60 s has nothing
to subtract from.

### A.3 What the APS returns

The APS calls the ADS, receives one ad, and converts the decision into the
linear resolution document — a `ListMPD` (§5.2).

```xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<MPD xmlns="urn:mpeg:dash:schema:mpd:2011"
     profiles="urn:mpeg:dash:profile:list:2024"
     type="list"
     minBufferTime="PT1S"
     publishTime="2026-09-18T12:00:03Z">

  <BaseURL>https://cdn.example.com/creatives/</BaseURL>

  <Period id="ad-01" duration="PT20S">
    <ImportedMPD earliestResolutionTimeOffset="0">creative-2201.mpd</ImportedMPD>
  </Period>

</MPD>
```

### A.4 The sub-MPD

`creative-2201.mpd`, under the Single-Period Static profile (§5.4), with the
tracking schedule the ADS chose (§5.5).

```xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>
<MPD xmlns="urn:mpeg:dash:schema:mpd:2011"
     profiles="urn:mpeg:dash:profile:sps:2024"
     type="static"
     minBufferTime="PT2S"
     publishTime="2026-09-18T12:00:03Z">

  <Period id="1" duration="PT20S">

    <EventStream schemeIdUri="urn:mpeg:dash:event:callback:2015"
                 value="1" timescale="1000">
      <Event presentationTime="0"     id="1">https://tracker.example.com/impression?ad=2201</Event>
      <Event presentationTime="0"     id="2">https://tracker.example.com/start?ad=2201</Event>
      <Event presentationTime="5000"  id="3">https://tracker.example.com/firstQuartile?ad=2201</Event>
      <Event presentationTime="10000" id="4">https://tracker.example.com/midpoint?ad=2201</Event>
      <Event presentationTime="15000" id="5">https://tracker.example.com/thirdQuartile?ad=2201</Event>
      <Event presentationTime="20000" id="6">https://tracker.example.com/complete?ad=2201</Event>
    </EventStream>

    <AdaptationSet contentType="video"
                   segmentAlignment="true" startWithSAP="1">
      <Representation id="v1" mimeType="video/mp4" codecs="avc1.4d401f"
                      bandwidth="2500000" width="1280" height="720">
        <BaseURL>media/creative-2201.mp4</BaseURL>
        <SegmentBase indexRange="0-912"/>
      </Representation>
    </AdaptationSet>

  </Period>
</MPD>
```

Beacon times are relative to this ad's own presentation, not to any period of
the main MPD, and each `<Event>` carries an `@id` because de-duplication inside
a candidate is keyed on it (§5.5).

### A.5 The walk-through

1. The Player parses the main MPD and finds event `101` in the insertion
   `<EventStream>`, at presentation time 0 with a cap of 30 000 ms.
2. It computes the Earliest Resolution Time:
   `ERT = 0 − 0 = 0 ms`. Resolution is admissible from the first instant of the
   session.
3. It issues `GET https://ads.example.com/aps/preroll?session_id=<value>`. The
   `session_id` parameter comes from the `<RequestParam>` declaration, whose
